Peak algebra in noncommuting variables Thanks: The second author was supported in part by the Provincial Nature Science Foundation of Shandong, Project No. ZR2024QA026 and the Fundamental Research Funds for the Central Universities.
Farid Aliniaeifard, Shu Xiao Li

Abstract
. The well-known descent-to-peak map Θ QSym \Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}} for the Hopf algebra of quasisymmetric functions, QSym \mathrm{QSym} , and the peak algebra Π \Pi were originally defined by Stembridge in 1997. We define the labelled descent-to-peak map Θ NCQSym \Theta_{{\mathrm{NCQSym}}} and extend the notion of the peak algebra to noncommuting variables. Moreover, we define Schur Q Q -functions in noncommuting variables having properties analogous to the classical Schur Q Q -functions.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
05E05, 05E10, 16T30, 20C30

1.
Introduction

The first comprehensive study on the combinatorics of peaks was conducted by Stembridge
[
48
]
, who developed and introduced enriched
(P,\gamma)
-partitions, which is an analouge to Stanley’s theory of
(P,\gamma)
-partitions, with the key distinction that the notion of peaks replaces the notion of descents in the context of linear extensions of posets.
Generating functions of
(P,\gamma)
-partitions are the fundamental functions,
F_{(P,\gamma)}
, which give the Hopf algebra of quasisymmetric functions
\mathrm{QSym}
, and the generating functions of enriched
(P,\gamma)
-partitions are the enriched fundamental functions,
K_{(P,\gamma)}
, which span the peak algebra
\Pi
. Stembridge also defined the descent-to-peak algebra morphism
\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}
from
\mathrm{QSym}
to
\Pi
where
F_{(P,\gamma)}
maps to
K_{(P,\gamma)}
, and showed that the dimension of the homogenous functions of degree
n
of
\Pi
,
\Pi_{n}
, is equal to the number of odd compositions, compositions whose all parts are odd, which is equal to Fibonacci number
f_{n}
. Moreover, he showed that restricting the map
\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}
to symmetric functions gives the Hopf algebra of Schur
Q
-functions. The Hopf algebra of Schur
Q
-functions, whose bases are indexed by odd partitions, are introduced in
[
43
]
to study the projective representations of symmetric and alternating groups. Combinatorially, the Schur
Q
-functions are equipped with a theory of shifted tableaux, including RSK correspondence, Littlewood-Richardson rule, and jeu de taquin
[
41
,
47
,
51
]
. In
[
9
]
, Bergeron et al. showed that the peak algebra is a Hopf algebra and also the map
\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}
is a Hopf algebra morphism. Also, the main result of
[
42
]
by Schocker is that the peak algebra is a left co-ideal of
\mathrm{QSym}
under internal coproduct.

One intriguing application of peak algebra is its role in providing a combinatorial formula for the Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials, as discussed in
[
17
]
. In their seminal paper
[
31
]
, Kazhdan and Lusztig introduced a set of polynomials that are indexed by pairs of elements in a Coxeter group
W
. These polynomials are now widely known as the Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials of
W
(see, for instance,
[
16
]
or
[
29
]
).
These polynomials play a fundamental role in several areas of mathematics, including representation theory, the geometry of Schubert varieties, the theory of Verma modules, Macdonald polynomials, and the Hodge theory of Soergel bimodules (see, for example,
[
5
,
15
,
18
,
20
,
21
,
24
,
25
,
26
,
32
,
49
]
). In
[
17
]
, Brenti and Caselli gave
new characterization of the peak subalgebra of the
algebra of quasisymmetric functions and used this characterization to construct
a new basis for the peak algebra and a combinatorial formula for the Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials.

It is also shown that the peak algebra corresponds to the representations of the
0
-Hecke-Clifford algebra
[
11
]
.
Further studies revealed connections between peaks and a variety of seemingly unrelated topics, such as the generalized Dehn-Sommerville equations
[
1
,
10
,
14
]
and the Schubert calculus of isotropic flag manifolds
[
10
,
13
]
. Notably, in
[
36
,
37
]
, the peak algebra is generalized to the Poirier-Reutenauer Hopf algebra of standard Young tableaux, which is introduced in
[
39
]
. Other generalizations can be found in
[
2
,
7
,
28
]
.

In this paper, we provide an elegant and natural definition of the peak algebra in noncommuting variables and extend the theta map. We also generalize many known results related to the peak algebra to noncommuting variables. The proofs we provide here differ from the commutative cases; as a result of our constructions, we give alternative and often shorter proofs. Our construction also establishes the definition of Schur
Q
-functions in noncommutative variables. In Section
2
we provide a summary of the results using generalized chromatic functions along with a compelling combinatorial description of the findings. This section also offers a clear presentation of the results in this paper without the proofs, which is self-contained, and it would be sufficient for a reader interested in results without proofs.
Section
3
gives the relevant background.
Section
4
describes set compositions and odd set compositions of
[n]=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
using pairs of subsets of
[n-1]
and permutations of
[n]
.
In Section
5
we extend the notion of the fundamental functions to noncommuting variables and in Proposition
5.5
prove an analogue of the fundamental theorem of
(P,\gamma)
-partitions.
In Section
6
we extend the notion of the enriched fundamental functions to noncommuting variables and in Proposition
6.3
prove an analog of the fundamental theorem of enriched
(P,\gamma)
-partitions.
In Section
7
, we introduce enriched monomial functions. In Thereom
7.5
, we show that enriched monomial functions give a basis for the peak algebra in noncommuting variables,
\mathrm{NC}\Pi
, and the dimension of the homogenous functions of degree
n
of
\mathrm{NC}\Pi
,
\mathrm{NC}\Pi_{n}
, is equal to the number of odd set compositions, set compositions whose all parts are odd, which is equal to
a_{n}
where
a_{n}
is the sequence A006154 in the OEIS.
In Section
8
, we show that
\mathrm{NC}\Pi
is a Hopf algebra and describe its product and coproduct in the language of generalized chromatic functions.
In Section
9
, we extend the notion of descent-to-peak map and define the labelled descent-to-peak map
\Theta_{\mathrm{NCQSym}}
, and give short proofs for the main results in
[
9
]
. In Proposition
9.2
, we show that
\Theta_{\mathrm{NCQSym}}
maps the monomial basis of
\mathrm{NCQSym}
to a constant multiple of the enriched monomial basis of
\mathrm{NC}\Pi
. In Theorem
9.4
, we find the value of
\Theta_{\mathrm{NCQSym}}
at fundamental functions and that applying the map
\rho
, where it commutes the variables, we obtain the descent-to-peak map
\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}
.
In Section
10
, we define the Hopf algebra of Schur
Q
-functions in noncommuting variables
\mathrm{NC}\Omega
and show that it is equal to the restriction of the map
\Theta_{\mathrm{NCQSym}}
to symmetric functions in noncommuting variables. The Schur
Q
-functions that are a basis for
\mathrm{NC}\Omega
are indexed by odd set partitions.
In Section
11
, we present several combinatorial properties of set compositions that we use them in the last section to show that the peak algebra in noncommuting variables is a left co-ideal of
\mathrm{NCQSym}
under internal coproduct, extending Schocker’s result in
[
43
]
that the peak algebra is a left co-ideal of
\mathrm{QSym}
under internal coproduct.

2.
A painted synopsis

We summarize the results of this paper using generalized chromatic functions
[
3
]
, providing a combinatorial perspective on the results.

2.1.
Edge-coloured digraphs and some operators

Stanley
[
45
]
defined
(P,\gamma)
-partitions by generalizing MacMahon’s work on plane partitions
[
34
]
1
1

1

For a complete history of
P
-partitions see I. M. Gessel’s paper
[
23
]
.
.
(P,\gamma)
-partitions can be identified as certain vertex-colourings of some family of edge-coloured digraphs. We describe this family of edge-coloured digraphs and some useful operators between them.

The Hasse diagram of a poset
P=(X,\leq)
can be seen as a digraph whose vertices are the elements of the ground set
X
of the poset, and there is a directed edge from
a
to
b
if
a\leq b
and if there is
c\in X
such that
a\leq c\leq b
, then either
c=a
or
c=b
.
Throughout this paper, all digraphs are Hasse diagrams of some posets.
For example, the following digraph corresponds to a poset with the ground set
\{1,2,3,4\}
.

4
2
1
3

An
edge-coloured digraph
is a digraph whose edges are of the form
\rightarrow
or
\Rightarrow
.

Figure 1.
An edge-coloured digraph

We now define some useful edge-coloured digraphs. Let

Q_{n}
(resp.
P_{n}
) be the edge-coloured directed path with
n
vertices whose all edges are of the form
\Rightarrow
(resp.
\rightarrow
).

Q_{3}

P_{3}

The
disjoint union
of edge-coloured digraphs
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
with
V(G_{1})\cap V(G_{2})=\emptyset
, denoted
G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
, is an edge-coloured digraph such that

(1)

The vertex set of
G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
is the disjoint union of the vertex sets of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
.

(2)

The edge set of
G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
is the disjoint union of the edge sets of
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
.

(3)

a\Rightarrow b
in
G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
if either
a\Rightarrow b
in
G_{1}
or in
G_{2}
.

(4)

a\rightarrow b
in
G_{1}\sqcup G_{2}
if either
a\rightarrow b
in
G_{1}
or in
G_{2}
.

Q_{3}\sqcup P_{3}

The
solid sum
of edge-coloured directed paths
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, denoted by
G_{1}\rightarrow G_{2}
, is an edge-coloured digraph obtained by connecting the last vertex of
G_{1}
to the first vertex of
G_{2}
by a solid edge
\rightarrow
.

Q_{3}\rightarrow Q_{3}

2.2.
Proper colourings and generalized chromatic functions

As we mentioned earlier,
(P,\gamma)
-partitions can be identified as certain types of vertex-colourings of edge-coloured digraphs. We describe these types of vertex-colourings of edge-coloured digraphs, and then we construct their generating functions, which are called generalized chromatic functions.

A
proper
colouring of an edge-coloured digraph
G
is a function

\kappa:V(G)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,3,\dots\}

such that

(1)

If
a\Rightarrow b
, then
\kappa(a)\leq\kappa(b)
.

(2)

If
a\rightarrow b
, then
\kappa(a)<\kappa(b)
.

Given an edge-coloured digraph, we usually write the colours of the vertices outside of the vertices, which are non-bold positive integers.

2
2
1
2

Figure 2.
A proper colouring of the edge-coloured digraph in Figure
1

Recall that
{\mathbb{Q}}[[x_{1},x_{2},\dots]]
is the algebra of formal power series in infinitely many commuting variables
x=\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots\}
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
.

The
generalized chromatic function
of an edge-coloured digraph
G
is

{\mathscr{X}}_{G}=\sum_{\kappa}\prod_{v\in V(G)}x_{\kappa(v)}

where the sum is over all proper colourings
\kappa
of
G
. For example, if
G
is the edge-coloured digraph in Figure
1
, then

{\mathscr{X}}_{G}=&x_{1}x_{2}^{3}&+&x_{1}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}&+&x_{1}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}&+&x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}^{2}&+\cdots.\end{array}

Generalized chromatic functions can be seen as generating functions of
(P,\gamma)
-partitions, and so they are quasisymmetric functions (see Section
3.3
).

2.3.
Enriched colourings and enriched chromatic functions

Stembridge
[
48
]
defined enriched
(P,\gamma)
-partitions and used them to associate tableaux with Schur
Q
-functions
[
43
]
2
2

2

For an English reference, see I. G. Macdonald’s book
[
33
, Chapter III, Section 8]
, where he described Schur’s
Q
-functions in more detail.
. Enriched
(P,\gamma)
-partitions can be identified as certain types of vertex-colourings of edge-coloured digraphs. We describe these types of vertex-colourings of edge-coloured digraphs, and then we construct their generating functions, which are called enriched chromatic functions.

Given an edge-coloured digraph
G
, an
enriched
colouring of
G
is a function

\kappa:V(G)\rightarrow\{\dots\prec-1\prec 1\prec-2\prec 2\prec\cdots\}

such that

(1)

If
a\Rightarrow b
, then either
\kappa(a)\prec\kappa(b)
or
\kappa(a)=\kappa(b)>0
.

(2)

If
a\rightarrow b
, then either
\kappa(a)\prec\kappa(b)
or
\kappa(a)=\kappa(b)<0
.

The
enriched chromatic function
of an edge-coloured digraph
G
is

{\mathscr{E}}_{G}=\sum_{\kappa}\prod_{v\in V(G)}x_{|\kappa(v)|}

where the sum is over all enriched colourings
\kappa
of
G
. For example, if
G
is the edge-coloured digraph in Figure
1
, then

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ccccccccc}&\hbox to61.85pt{\vbox to85.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 31.37227pt\lower-7.71318pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.54 10.67 L 21.59 26.98}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.54 10.67 L 21.59 26.98}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.62473}{0.78084}{-0.78084}{0.62473}{15.60188pt}{19.50116pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.9 5.2 C -3.58 3.25 0 0.33 0.98 0 C 0 -0.33 -3.58 -3.25 -3.9 -5.2}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

2.4.
Peak algebra, descent-to-peak map, and earlier results

Stembridge defined the peak algebra
\Pi
3
3

3

See Section
3.8
for the justification that this space is called the peak algebra.
in
[
48
]
as a space spanned by the generating functions of enriched
(P,\gamma)
-partitions. Since generating functions of enriched
(P,\gamma)
-partitions are the enriched chromatic functions of edge-coloured digraphs and vice versa (Lemma
3.8
), we have that the peak algebra is spanned by the set

\{{\mathscr{E}}_{G}:\text{$G$ is an edge-coloured digraph}\}.

Stembridge also defined the
descent-to-peak map
4
4

4

See Section
3.3
for the justification that this map is called descent-to-peak map.

\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}
; as shown in Corollary
3.9
, we can write it as follows,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cccc}\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}:&\mathrm{QSym}&\rightarrow&\Pi\\
&{\mathscr{X}}_{G}&\mapsto&{\mathscr{E}}_{G}.\end{array}

He showed that the descent-to-peak map is a surjective algebra morphism
[
48
, Theorem 3.1]
.

We now present a series of known results for peak algebra and the descent-to-peak map that we later extend to noncommuting variables.

(1)

A
composition

\alpha
of
n
, denoted
\alpha\vDash n
, is a list of positive integers whose sum is
n
. An
odd composition
is a composition whose all parts are odd. A set
B\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}
is called a
peak set
5
5

5

The
peak set of a permutation

\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
is the set
\mathrm{Peak}(\sigma)=\{i\in\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}:\sigma(i-1)<\sigma(i)>\sigma(i+1)\}
. If
i\in\mathrm{Peak}(\sigma)
, then
i-1,i+1\not\in\mathrm{Peak}(\sigma)
. Thus each peak set is the peak set of a permutation.
if
b\in B
implies that
\{b-1,b+1\}\cap B=\emptyset
. By
[
48
, Theorem 3.1]
the dimension of the space of the homogeneous elements of degree
n
of
\Pi
,
\Pi_{n}
, is

\displaystyle\dim(\Pi_{n})

\displaystyle=|\{B\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}\text{~is a peak set}\}|

\displaystyle=|\{\alpha\vDash[n]:\text{$\alpha$ is an odd composition}\}|

\displaystyle=f_{n},

where
f_{n}
is the
n
th Fibonacci number.

(2)

The peak algebra
\Pi
is a Hopf algebra
[
9
, Theorem 2.2]
.

(3)

The descent-to-peak map,
\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}
, is a Hopf algebra morphism
[
9
, Section 2]
.

(4)

The value of the descent-to-peak map at the monomial and fundamental bases elements of
\mathrm{QSym}
are as follows.

(a)

Fundamental basis.

Given a subset
A=\{a_{1}<a_{2}<\cdots<a_{k}\}
of
[n-1]
, the
fundamental
basis element
F_{A}
of
\mathrm{QSym}
is the generalized chromatic function of the following edge-coloured digraph,

Q_{a_{1}}\rightarrow Q_{a_{2}-a_{1}}\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow Q_{n-a_{k}}.

Therefore, by the expression of the descent-to-peak map
\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}
in (
2.4
) we have

\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}(F_{A})=\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}({\mathscr{X}}_{Q_{a_{1}}\rightarrow Q_{a_{2}-a_{1}}\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow Q_{n-a_{k}}})={\mathscr{E}}_{Q_{a_{1}}\rightarrow Q_{a_{2}-a_{1}}\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow Q_{n-a_{k}}}.

(b)

Monomial basis.
Given a subset
A=\{a_{1}<a_{2}<\cdots<a_{k}\}
of
[n-1]
, the
monomial
basis element
M_{A}
of
\mathrm{QSym}
is

M_{A}=\sum_{C\subseteq A}(-1)^{|A|-|C|}F_{C}.

Given a peak set
B\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}
, the
monomial peak function

\eta_{B}
is

\eta_{B}=(-1)^{|B|}\sum_{A\subseteq\mathrm{Odd}(B)}2^{|A|+1}M_{A}

where

\mathrm{Odd}(B)=[n-1]\setminus(B\cup(B-1)).

Let
A=\{a_{1}<a_{2}<\cdots<a_{k}\}\subseteq[n-1]
where
n-a_{k}
is odd. Then there is a unique peak set
B\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}
such that

\mathrm{Odd}(B)=\mathrm{ODiff}(A)

where

\mathrm{ODiff}(A)=\{a_{i}:1\leq i\leq k,a_{i}-a_{i-1}\text{ is odd}\}
with
a_{0}=0
.
By
[
1
, Equation (4.9)]
, we have

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}(M_{A})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(-1)^{n-1-|B|-|A|}\eta_{B}&\text{if $n-\max(A)$ is odd,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(5)

A
partition

\lambda
of
n
, denoted
\lambda\vdash n
, is a non-increasing composition of
n
. The values of the descent-to-peak map at different bases of
\mathrm{Sym}
are as follows.

(a)

For
\lambda=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\dots\lambda_{\ell(\lambda)}
,
by
[
3
, Table 2]
we have that the
complete homogeneous symmetric function

h_{\lambda}
(resp.,
elementary symmetric function

e_{\lambda}
) is the generalized chromatic function of

Q_{\lambda}=Q_{\lambda_{1}}\sqcup Q_{\lambda_{2}}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup Q_{\lambda_{\ell(\lambda)}},~~~{\text{(resp.}~P_{\lambda}=P_{\lambda_{1}}\sqcup P_{\lambda_{2}}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{\lambda_{\ell(\lambda)}})}.

Thus by the description of the descent-to-peak map in (
2.4
)

\Theta_{\mathrm{Sym}}(h_{\lambda})={\mathscr{E}}_{Q_{\lambda}}~~~\text{(resp. $\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}(e_{\lambda})={\mathscr{E}}_{P_{\lambda}}$)}.

(b)

For
\lambda=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\cdots\lambda_{\ell(\lambda)}\vdash n
, the
Schur function

s_{\lambda}
is

s_{\lambda}=\det\left({\mathscr{X}}_{Q_{\lambda_{i}-i+j}}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq\ell(\lambda)}.

By the description of the descent-to-peak map in (
2.4
)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathrm{Sym}}(s_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\Theta_{\mathrm{Sym}}(\det\left({\mathscr{X}}_{Q_{\lambda_{i}-i+j}}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq\ell(\lambda)})

\displaystyle=\det\left({\mathscr{E}}_{Q_{\lambda_{i}-i+j}}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq\ell(\lambda)}.

(c)

For
\lambda=\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\dots\lambda_{\ell(\lambda)}\vdash n
, we have the
power sum symmetric function

p_{\lambda}
is

p_{\lambda}=\left(\sum_{i=1}x_{i}^{\lambda_{1}}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}x_{i}^{\lambda_{2}}\right)\cdots\left(\sum_{i=1}x_{i}^{\lambda_{\ell(\lambda)}}\right).

By
[
33
, Chapter III, Example 10]

\Theta_{\mathrm{Sym}}(p_{\lambda})=\begin{cases}2^{\ell(\lambda)}p_{\lambda}&\text{if $\lambda$ is odd,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(6)

For a partition
\lambda
, the
Schur
Q
-function
is

q_{\lambda}={\mathscr{E}}_{Q_{\lambda}}={\mathscr{E}}_{P_{\lambda}}.

The Hopf algebra of
Schur
Q
-functions
introduced in
[
43
]
is denoted by
\Omega
and

\Omega=\mathbb{C}\text{-span}\{q_{\lambda}:\lambda\text{~is an odd partition}\}.

Moreover,

\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}(\mathrm{Sym})=\Omega.

By
[
48
, Theorem 3.8]
,
\Omega
is the intersection of the Peak algebra
\Pi
and the Hopf algebra of symmetric functions
\mathrm{Sym}
, that is,

\Omega=\Pi\cap\mathrm{Sym}.

The restriction of the descent-to-peak map
\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}
to
\mathrm{Sym}
is denoted by
\Theta_{\mathrm{Sym}}
. By
[
48
, Theorem 3.1]
the following diagram commutes.
\mathsf{\mathrm{Sym}}
\mathrm{QSym}
\Omega
\Pi
\iota
\iota
\Theta_{\mathrm{Sym}}
\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}
{\mathscr{X}}_{G}
{\mathscr{E}}_{G}

(7)

In
[
43
]
Schocker proves that the peak algebra is a left co-ideal of
\mathrm{QSym}
under internal coproduct.

2.5.
Generalized chromatic functions in noncommuting variables

A
labelled edge-coloured digraph
is an edge-coloured digraph where its vertex set is a subset of
\mathbb{N}
. We usually denote the vertices of a labelled edge-coloured digraph by bold positive integers.

\bf 1
\bf 2
\bf 3
\bf 4

Figure 3.
A labelled edge-coloured digraph

We usually use
{\mathbf{G}}
to denote a labelled edge-coloured digraph whose underlying edge-coloured digraph is
G
. For example, if we denote the labelled edge-coloured digraph in Figure
3
by
{\mathbf{G}}
, then
G
is the edge-coloured digraph in Figure
1
.

We now define some useful labelled edge-coloured digraphs. For any set

S=\{i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{k}\}

of positive integers, let

Q_{S}
(resp.
P_{S}
) be the labelled edge-coloured directed path with vertex set
S
such that its coloured edges are
{i_{j}}\Rightarrow{i_{j+1}}
(resp.
{i_{j}}\rightarrow{i_{j+1}}
) for
1\leq j\leq k
.

{\bf 3}
5
8
Q_{\{3,5,8\}}

{\bf 3}
5
8
P_{\{3,5,8\}}

The
disjoint union
of labelled edge-coloured digraphs
{\mathbf{G}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{G}}_{2}
with
V({\mathbf{G}}_{1})\cap V({\mathbf{G}}_{2})=\emptyset
, is denoted by
{\mathbf{G}}_{1}\sqcup{\mathbf{G}}_{2}
.

\bf 3
\bf 5
\bf 8
\bf 2
\bf 6
\bf 9
Q_{\{3,5,8\}}\sqcup P_{\{2,6,9\}}

The
solid sum
of labelled edge-coloured directed paths
{\mathbf{G}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{G}}_{2}
, denoted
{\mathbf{G}}_{1}\rightarrow{\mathbf{G}}_{2}
, is an edge-coloured digraph obtained by connecting the last vertex of
{\mathbf{G}}_{1}
to the first vertex of
{\mathbf{G}}_{2}
by a solid edge
\rightarrow
.

{\bf 3}
5
8
Q_{\{3,5,8\}}\rightarrow Q_{\{2,6,9\}}
{\bf 2}
6
9

Recall that
{\mathbb{Q}}\langle\langle{\bf x}_{1},{\bf x}_{2},\dots\rangle\rangle
is the algebra of formal power series in infinitely many noncommuting variables
{\bf x}=\{{\bf x}_{1},{\bf x}_{2},\dots\}
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
.

The
generalized chromatic function in noncommuting variables
of a labelled edge-coloured digraph
{\mathbf{G}}
with vertex set
[n]
is

{\mathscr{Y}}_{\mathbf{G}}=\sum_{\kappa}{\bf x}_{\kappa(1)}{\bf x}_{\kappa(2)}\dots{\bf x}_{\kappa(n)}

where the sum is over all proper colourings
\kappa
of
G
. For example, if
{\mathbf{G}}
is the labelled edge-coloured digraph in Figure
3

{\mathscr{Y}}_{G}=&{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{2}{\bf x}_{2}{\bf x}_{2}&+&{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{2}{\bf x}_{2}{\bf x}_{3}&+&{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{2}{\bf x}_{3}{\bf x}_{2}&+&{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{2}{\bf x}_{3}{\bf x}_{3}&+\cdots.\end{array}

Remark 2.1
.

The generalized chromatic functions in noncommuting variables first appeared in
[
3
]
.

2.6.
Enriched chromatic functions in noncommuting variables

The
enriched chromatic function in noncommuting variables
of a labelled edge-coloured digraph
{\mathbf{G}}
with vertex set
[n]
is

{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathbf{G}}=\sum_{\kappa}{\bf x}_{|\kappa(1)|}{\bf x}_{|\kappa(2)|}\dots{\bf x}_{|\kappa(n)|}

where the sum is over all enriched colouring
\kappa
of
G
. For example, if
{\mathbf{G}}
is the labelled edge-coloured digraph in Figure
3
, then

{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathbf{G}}=&{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{1}&+&{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{1}&+&{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{2}{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{2}&+&{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{2}{\bf x}_{2}{\bf x}_{2}&+\cdots.\end{array}

2.7.
Peak algebra in noncommuting variables and labelled descent-to-peak map

In this section, we show that all earlier definitions and results in Section
2.4
can be generalized to noncommuting variables.

The
peak algebra in noncommuting variables
, denoted
\mathrm{NC}\Pi
, is the space spanned by

\{{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathbf{G}}:\text{${\mathbf{G}}$ is a labelled edge-coloured digraph}\}.

The
labelled descent-to-peak
map
\Theta_{\mathrm{NCQSym}}
is

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cccc}\Theta_{\mathrm{NCQSym}}:&\mathrm{NCQSym}&\rightarrow&\mathrm{NC}\Pi\\
&{\mathscr{Y}}_{\mathbf{G}}&\mapsto&{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathbf{G}}.\end{array}

(1)

A
set composition

\phi
of
[n]
, denoted
\phi\vDash[n]
, is a sequence of mutually disjoint nonempty sets whose union is
[n]
. An
odd set composition
is a set composition whose all blocks have odd sizes. We show in Theorem
7.5
that the dimension of the space of homogeneous elements of degree
n
of
\mathrm{NC}\Pi
,
\mathrm{NC}\Pi_{n}
, is

\displaystyle\dim(\mathrm{NC}\Pi_{n})

\displaystyle=|\{(B,\sigma):B\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}\text{~is a peak set and~}\mathrm{Des}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathrm{Odd}(B)\}|

\displaystyle=|\{\phi\vDash[n]:\text{$\phi$ is an odd set composition}\}|

\displaystyle=a_{n}

where

\mathrm{Des}(\sigma)=\{i\in[n-1]:\sigma(i)>\sigma(i+1)\},
and
a_{n}
is the sequence A006154 in the OEIS
[
38
]
.

(2)

In Section
8
, we study the structure of
\mathrm{NC}\Pi
and show that it is a Hopf algebra.

(3)

In Theorem
9.4
, we show that the labelled descent-to-peak map,

\Theta_{\mathrm{NCQSym}}
, is a surjective Hopf algebra morphism and the following diagram commutes.

\mathsf{\mathrm{NCQSym}}
\mathrm{QSym}
\mathrm{NC}\Pi
\Pi
\rho
\rho
\Theta_{\mathrm{NCQSym}}
\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}
{\mathscr{Y}}_{\mathbf{G}}
{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathbf{G}}
{\mathscr{X}}_{G}
{\mathscr{E}}_{G}

(4)

Given a subset
A=\{a_{1}<a_{2}<\cdots<a_{k}\}
of
[n-1]

and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, we say
(A,\sigma)
is
standard
if
\mathrm{Des}(\sigma)\subseteq A.
The value of the labelled descent-to-peak map at the monomial and fundamental bases elements of
\mathrm{NCQSym}
are as follows.

(a)

Fundamental basis.

Let
A=\{a_{1}<a_{2}<\cdots<a_{k}\}
of
[n-1]

and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that
(A,\sigma)
is standard. The
fundamental
basis element
\mathbf{F}_{(A,\sigma)}
of
\mathrm{NCQSym}
is the generalized chromatic function in noncommuting variables of the following labelled edge-coloured digraph,

{\mathbf{G}}=Q_{\{\sigma(1),\dots,\sigma(a_{1})\}}\rightarrow Q_{\{\sigma(a_{1}+1),\dots,\sigma(a_{2})\}}\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow Q_{\{\sigma(a_{k}+1),\dots,\sigma(n)\}}.

Therefore, by the description of the labelled descent-to-peak map in (
2.7
)

\Theta_{\mathrm{NCQSym}}(\mathbf{F}_{(A,\sigma)})=\Theta_{\mathrm{NCQSym}}({\mathscr{Y}}_{{\mathbf{G}}})={\mathscr{F}}_{\mathbf{G}}.

(b)

Monomial basis.
Let
A=\{a_{1}<a_{2}<\cdots<a_{k}\}
of
[n-1]

and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that
(A,\sigma)
is standard. The
monomial
basis element
\mathbf{M}_{(A,\sigma)}
of
\mathrm{NCQSym}
is

\mathbf{M}_{(A,\sigma)}=\sum_{C\subseteq A}(-1)^{|A|-|C|}\mathbf{F}_{(C,\sigma)}.

For each peak set
B\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}
, the
monomial peak function in noncommuting variables

{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{(B,\sigma)}
is

{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{(B,\sigma)}=(-1)^{|B|}\sum_{A\subseteq\mathrm{Odd}(B)}2^{|A|+1}\mathbf{M}_{(A,\sigma)}.

In Proposition
9.2
we show that

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}(\mathbf{M}_{(A,\sigma)})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(-1)^{n-1-|B|-|A|}{\boldsymbol{\eta}}_{(B,\sigma)}&\text{if $n-\max(A)$ is odd,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(5)

A
set partition

\pi
of
[n]
is the set of mutually disjoint non-empty subsets
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\dots,\pi_{\ell(\pi)}
of
[n]
whose their union is
[n]
. We denote it by
\pi=\pi_{1}/\pi_{2}/\cdots/\pi_{\ell(\pi)}\vdash[n]
.

For a set
S
, let
\mathfrak{S}_{S}
be the set of all bijections from
S
to
S
. Given a labelled edge-coloured digraph
{\mathbf{G}}
with vertex set
S
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{S}
, define
\sigma\circ{\mathbf{G}}
to be the labelled edge-coloured digraph with vertex set
S
in which

•

i\Rightarrow j
in
{\mathbf{G}}
if and only if
{\sigma(i)}\Rightarrow{\sigma(j)}
in
\sigma\circ{\mathbf{G}}
.

•

i\rightarrow j
in
{\mathbf{G}}
if and only if
{\sigma(i)}\rightarrow{\sigma(j)}
in
\sigma\circ{\mathbf{G}}
.

The values of the labelled descent-to-peak map at different bases of
\mathrm{NCSym}
are as follows.

(a)

For
\pi=\pi_{1}/\pi_{2}/\cdots/\pi_{\ell(\pi)}\vdash[n]
, by
[
3
, Section 10]
, we have that the
complete homogeneous symmetric function in noncommuting variables

{\mathbf{h}}_{\pi}
(resp.,
elementary symmetric function in noncommuting variables

{\mathbf{e}}_{\pi}
) is

{\mathbf{h}}_{\pi}=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\pi}}{\mathscr{Y}}_{\sigma\circ Q_{\pi}},~~~{\text{(resp.}}~{\mathbf{e}}_{\pi}=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\pi}}{\mathscr{Y}}_{\sigma\circ P_{\pi}}),

where

Q_{\pi}=Q_{\pi_{1}}\sqcup Q_{\pi_{2}}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup Q_{\pi_{\ell(\pi)}},
(resp.
P_{\pi}=P_{\pi_{1}}\sqcup P_{\pi_{2}}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{\pi_{\ell(\pi)}}
)
and
\mathfrak{S}_{\pi}=\mathfrak{S}_{\pi_{1}}\times\mathfrak{S}_{\pi_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mathfrak{S}_{\pi_{\ell(\pi)}}.

Thus by (
2.7
),

\Theta_{\mathrm{NCSym}}({\mathbf{h}}_{\pi})=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\pi}}{\mathscr{F}}_{\sigma\circ Q_{\pi}}~~~\text{(resp.}~\Theta_{\mathrm{NCSym}}({\mathbf{e}}_{\pi})=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\pi}}{\mathscr{F}}_{\sigma\circ P_{\pi}}).

As for each
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{\pi}
,
{\mathscr{F}}_{\sigma\circ Q_{\pi}}={\mathscr{F}}_{\sigma\circ P_{\pi}}
, we conclude that

(2.5)

\Theta_{\mathrm{NCSym}}({\mathbf{h}}_{\pi})=\Theta_{\mathrm{NCSym}}({\mathbf{e}}_{\pi}).

(b)

For
\pi=\pi_{1}/\pi_{2}/\cdots/\pi_{\ell(\pi)}\vdash[n]
, define the permutation
\delta_{\pi}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that
if
\delta_{\pi}(i)\in\pi_{s}
and
\delta_{\pi}(i+1)\in\pi_{t}
, then either
s=t
and
\delta_{\pi}(i)<\delta_{\pi}(i+1)
or
\min(\pi_{s})<\min(\pi_{t})
. For example, if
\pi=156/24/36
, then
\delta_{\pi}=1562436
.
The
Schur function in noncommuting variable

{\mathbf{s}}_{\pi}
is

{\mathbf{s}}_{\pi}=\delta_{\pi}\circ{\mathbf{det}}\left({\mathscr{Y}}_{Q_{[\lambda_{i}-i+j]}}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq\ell(\pi)}.

Thus by (
2.7
),

\Theta_{\mathrm{NCSym}}({\mathbf{s}}_{\pi})=\delta_{\pi}\circ{\mathbf{det}}\left({\mathscr{F}}_{Q_{[\lambda_{i}-i+j]}}\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq\ell(\pi)}.

(c)

For
\pi=\pi_{1}/\pi_{2}/\cdots/\pi_{\ell(\pi)}\vdash[n]
, by Theorem
10.2
we have

\Theta_{\mathrm{NCSym}}({\mathbf{p}}_{\pi})=\begin{cases}2^{\ell(\pi)}{\mathbf{p}}_{\pi}&\text{if all blocks of $\pi$ have odd sizes,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(6)

For an odd set partition
\pi
, the
Schur
Q
-function in noncommuting variables
is

\mathbf{q}_{\pi}=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\pi}}{\mathscr{F}}_{\sigma\circ Q_{\pi}}=\sum_{\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{\pi}}{\mathscr{F}}_{\sigma\circ P_{\pi}}.

The Hopf algebra of
Schur Q-functions in noncommuting variables
is denoted by
\mathrm{NC}\Omega
and by Theorem
10.7
,

\mathrm{NC}\Omega=\mathbb{C}\text{-span}\{\mathbf{q}_{\pi}:\pi\text{~is an odd set partition}\}.

and by the action of
\Theta_{\mathrm{NCSym}}
on
\mathbf{h}_{\pi}
, we have

\mathrm{NC}\Omega=\Theta_{\mathrm{NCQSym}}(\mathrm{NCSym}).

By Theorem
10.5
,

\mathrm{NC}\Omega=\mathrm{NC}\Pi\cap\mathrm{NCSym}.

The restriction of the labelled descent-to-peak map
\Theta_{\mathrm{NCQSym}}
to
\mathrm{NCSym}
is denoted by
\Theta_{\mathrm{NCSym}}
. By Corollary
9.5
the following diagram commutes.

\mathsf{\mathrm{NCSym}}
\mathrm{NCQSym}
\mathrm{NC}\Omega
\mathrm{NC}\Pi
\iota
\iota
\Theta_{\mathrm{NCSym}}
\Theta_{\mathrm{NCQSym}}
{\mathscr{Y}}_{\mathbf{G}}
{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathbf{G}}

More generally, the following diagram commutes.

{\mathrm{Sym}}
\mathrm{QSym}
\Omega
\Pi
\mathrm{NCSym}
\mathrm{NCQSym}
\mathrm{NC}\Omega
\mathrm{NC}\Pi
\iota
\iota
\Theta_{\mathrm{Sym}}
\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}
\iota
\iota
\Theta_{\mathrm{NCSym}}
\Theta_{\mathrm{NCQSym}}
{\mathscr{X}}_{G}
{\mathscr{E}}_{G}
{\mathscr{Y}}_{\mathbf{G}}
{\mathscr{F}}_{\mathbf{G}}

(7)

In Section
12
, we show that the peak algebra in noncommuting variables is a left co-ideal of
\mathrm{NCQSym}
under internal coproduct. We give a combinatorial description of the structure constants, and our result implies Schocker’s result on
\mathrm{QSym}
.

3.
Preliminaries

3.1.
Compositions

Given a composition
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{k}),
each
\alpha_{i}
is called a
part
of
\alpha
, the
size
of
\alpha
is
|\alpha|=\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\cdots+\alpha_{k}
, and the
length
of
\alpha
is
k
. By convention, we denote by
\emptyset
the unique composition of size and length zero. For example,
(2,1,2)
is a composition of
5
with length
3
.

For every
A=\{a_{1}<a_{2}<\cdots<a_{k}\}\subseteq[n-1]
, define

{\rm Comp}(A)=(a_{1},a_{2}-a_{1},\dots,n-a_{k})\vDash[n].

For example, if
A=\{2,3\}\subseteq[5]
, then

{\rm Comp}(A)=(2,1,3).

The map

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\{A:A\subseteq[n-1]\}&\rightarrow&\{\alpha:\alpha\vDash[n]\}\\
A&\mapsto&{\rm Comp}(A)\end{array}

is a bijection.

3.2.
Odd compositions and peak sets

Recall that an odd composition is a composition whose all parts are odd. We say a subset
A=\{a_{1}<a_{2}<\dots<a_{k}\}
of
[n-1]
is an
odd set
if for each
1\leq i\leq k+1
,

a_{i}-a_{i-1}\text{~is an odd number,}

where
a_{0}=0
and
a_{k+1}=n.
Therefore, for any odd set
A
,
{\rm Comp}(A)
is an odd composition.

As we mentioned earlier, a set
B\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}
is called a peak set if
b\in B
implies that
\{b-1,b+1\}\cap B=\emptyset
. For any peak set
B\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}
, define

{\mathrm{Odd}}(B)=[n-1]\setminus(B\cup(B-1))

where
B-1=\{b-1:b\in B\}.

For example, if
B=\{2,5,7,10\}\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,10\}
, then

\mathrm{Odd}(B)=\{3,8\}.

Proposition 3.1
.

The map

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\{B\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}:\text{~$B$ is a peak set}\}&\rightarrow&\{A\subseteq[n-1]:\text{~$A$ is an odd set}\}\\
B&\mapsto&\mathrm{Odd}(B).\end{array}

is a bijection.

Proof.

Let
B\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}
be a peak set, and let

\mathrm{Odd}(B)=[n-1]\setminus(B\cup(B-1))=\{a_{1}<a_{2}<\dots<a_{k}\}.

Set
a_{0}=0
. Consider that for
1\leq i\leq k
, if
a_{i-1}\neq a_{i}-1
, then
a_{i-1}+1\in(B-1)
and
a_{i}-1\in B
(otherwise,
a_{i-1}\in B
or
a_{i}\in B
).

Since
B
is a peak set, each of
B
and
B-1
does not include a pair of consecutive integers. Therefore, the sequence

a_{i-1}+1,a_{i-1}+2,\ldots,a_{i}-2,a_{i}-1

is a sequence whose elements are alternatively in
B-1
and
B
, that is

a_{i-1}+1\in(B-1),a_{i-1}+2\in B,\ldots,a_{i}-2\in(B-1),a_{i}-1\in B.

Therefore, there are an even number of integers between
a_{i}
and
a_{i-1}
, and so their difference
a_{i}-a_{i-1}
is odd. This shows that
\mathrm{Odd}(B)
is an odd set and the function
\mathrm{Odd}
is well-defined.

The injectivity of the map
\mathrm{Odd}
is clear from the definition and now we show that
\mathrm{Odd}
is bijective. Given an odd set
A=\{a_{1}<a_{2}<\dots<a_{k}\}\subseteq[n-1]
, let

B=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k+1}\{a_{i-1}+2t:t\in\mathbb{N},a_{i}+2t\leq a_{i}-1\}

where
a_{0}=0
and
a_{k+1}=n.
For example, if
A=\{3,8\}\subseteq[10]
, then
B=\{2,5,7,10\}.

We have

\mathrm{Odd}(B)=A,

which gives the inverse of
\mathrm{Odd}
.
∎

For any peak set
B
, define

{\rm CompOdd}(B)={\rm Comp}({\rm Odd}(B)).

For example, if
B=\{2,5,7,10\}\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,10\}
, then

{\rm CompOdd}(B)={\rm Comp}(\{3,8\})=(3,5,3).

Since always
{\rm CompOdd}(B)
is an odd composition for any peak set
B
, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 3.2
.

The map

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\{B\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}:\text{~$B$ is a peak set}\}&\rightarrow&\{\alpha\vDash n:\text{~$\alpha$ is odd}\}\\
B&\mapsto&{\rm Comp}\mathrm{Odd}(B)\end{array}

is a bijection.

3.3.
P
-partitions, enriched
P
-partitions, fundamental functions, and the peak algebra

The notation and definitions in this section mainly follow from
[
48
]
.

Throughout this paper,
P=(X,\leq)
denotes a partially ordered set of size
n
with the ground set
X
. A
linear extension
of
P
is a total order
w=(w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{n})
of the ground set
X
such that
w_{i}<w_{j}
implies
i<j
. The set of linear extensions of
P
is denoted by
\mathcal{L}(P)
. A
labelled poset
is a pair
(P,\gamma)
where
P
is a poset with ground set
X
and
\gamma:X\to[n]
is a bijection.

Let
(P,\gamma)
be a labelled poset. A
(P,\gamma)
-partition
is a map

f:X\to\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,3,\dots\}

such that for all
a,b\in X
with
a\leq b
, we have:

(1)

f(a)\leq f(b)
.

(2)

If
\gamma(a)>\gamma(b)
, then
f(a)<f(b)
.

The set of all
(P,\gamma)
-partitions is denoted by
\mathcal{A}(P,\gamma)
.

3.4.
Quasisymmetric functions

Given
A\subseteq[n-1]
, define the
monomial quasisymmetric function

M_{A}
to be

M_{A}=\sum x_{i_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}x_{i_{2}}^{\alpha_{2}}\dots x_{i_{k}}^{\alpha_{k}}

where
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{k})={\rm Comp}(A)
and the sum is over all tuples
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{k})
of positive integers such that
i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{k}
.

The Hopf algebra of
quasisymmetric functions
is

\mathrm{QSym}=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\mathrm{QSym}_{n}

where

\mathrm{QSym}_{n}=\mathbb{Q}\text{-span}\{M_{A}:A\subseteq[n-1]\}.

The basis
\{M_{A}\}
is called the
monomial basis
of the Hopf algebra of quasisymmetric functions.

3.5.
Fundamental functions

Given a labelled poset
(P,\gamma)
with ground set
X
, define the
fundamental function

F_{(P,\gamma)}
to be

F_{(P,\gamma)}=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{A}(P,\gamma)}\prod_{a\in X}x_{f(a)}.

Example 3.3
.

P
be the following poset

a

F_{(P,\gamma)}=&x_{1}x_{2}^{3}&+&x_{1}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}&+&x_{1}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}&+&x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}^{2}&+\cdots.\end{array}

The following is known as the fundamental theorem of
(P,\gamma)
-partitions.

Theorem 3.4
.

[
46
, Lemma 3.15.3]

Let
(P,\gamma)
be a labelled poset. Then

\mathcal{A}(P,\gamma)=\bigsqcup_{w\in\mathcal{L}(P)}\mathcal{A}(w,\gamma).

Consequently,

F_{(P,\gamma)}=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{L}(P)}F_{(w,\gamma)}.

Given
w=w_{1}w_{2}\dots w_{n}\in\mathcal{L}(P)
,

\mathrm{Des}(w,\gamma)=\{i\in\{1,2,\dots,n-1\}:\gamma(w_{i})>\gamma(w_{i+1})\}.

For
w\in\mathcal{L}(P)
, the function
F_{(w,\gamma)}
only depends on the descent set
\mathrm{Des}(w,\gamma)
(see
[
48
, Section 2.2]
). That is,
F_{(w,\gamma)}=F_{(w,\gamma^{\prime})}
if and only if
\mathrm{Des}(w,\gamma)=\mathrm{Des}(w,\gamma^{\prime})
. Given
A\subseteq[n-1]
, define

F_{A}=F_{(w,\gamma)}

where
(w,\gamma)
is any labelled chain with
\mathrm{Des}(w,\gamma)=A
.

The set
\{F_{A}:A\subseteq[n-1]\}
is a basis for
\mathrm{QSym}_{n}
, and the set
\{F_{A}\}
is called the
fundamental basis
of the Hopf algebra of quasisymmetric functions. We have that

F_{A}=\sum_{A\subseteq C\subseteq[n-1]}M_{C}.

Example 3.5
.

Let
w=(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4},w_{5})
be a chain, and

\gamma(w_{1})\gamma(w_{2})\gamma(w_{3})\gamma(w_{4})\gamma(w_{5})=34152,

then

\mathrm{Des}(w,\gamma)=\{2,4\},

and so

F_{\{2,4\}}=F_{(w,\gamma)}.

Let
(P,\gamma)
be a labelled poset. An
enriched
(P,\gamma)
-partition
is a map

f:X\to\{\dots\prec-1\prec 1\prec-2\prec 2\prec\cdots\}

such that for all pairs
a,b\in X
with
a\leq b
, we have:

(1)

f(a)\preceq f(b)
.

(2)

f(a)=f(b)>0
implies
\gamma(a)<\gamma(b)
.

(3)

f(a)=f(b)<0
implies
\gamma(a)>\gamma(b)
.

The set of all enriched
(P,\gamma)
-partitions is denoted by
\mathcal{B}(P,\gamma)
.

Given a labelled poset
(P,\gamma)
with ground set
X
, define the
enriched fundamental function

K_{(P,\gamma)}
to be

K_{(P,\gamma)}=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{B}(P,\gamma)}\prod_{a\in X}x_{|f(a)|}.

K_{(P,\gamma)}=&x_{1}^{4}&+&x_{1}^{4}&+&x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}&+&x_{1}x_{2}^{3}&+\cdots.\end{array}

The following is the analogue of the fundamental theorem for enriched
P
-partitions.

Theorem 3.7
.

[
48
, Lemma 2.1]

Let
(P,\gamma)
be a labelled poset. Then

\mathcal{B}(P,\gamma)=\bigsqcup_{w\in\mathcal{L}(P)}\mathcal{B}(w,\gamma).

Consequently,

K_{(P,\gamma)}=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{L}(P)}K_{(w,\gamma)}.

Given
w\in\mathcal{L}(P)
,

\mathrm{Peak}(w,\gamma)=\{i:\gamma(w_{i-1})<\gamma(w_{i})>\gamma(w_{i+1})\}

is a peak set, and also any peak set is equal to some
\mathrm{Peak}(w,\gamma)
.

For
w\in\mathcal{L}(P)
, the function
K_{(w,\gamma)}
only depends on the peak set
\mathrm{Peak}(w,\gamma)
(see
[
48
, Section 2.2]
). That is,
K_{(w,\gamma)}=K_{(w,\gamma^{\prime})}
if and only if
\mathrm{Peak}(w,\gamma)=\mathrm{Peak}(w,\gamma^{\prime})
. Given a peak set
B\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}
, define

K_{B}=K_{(w,\gamma)}

where
(w,\gamma)
is any labelled chain with
\mathrm{Peak}(w,\gamma)=B
.

The
peak algebra
is

\Pi=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\Pi_{n}

where

\Pi_{n}={\mathbb{Q}}\text{-span}\{K_{B}:B\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}\text{~is a peak set}\}.

The basis
\{K_{B}:B\text{~is a peak set}\}
is called the
enriched fundamental basis
of the peak algebra.

Given a peak set
B\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}
, define the
enriched monomial function
to be

\eta_{B}=\sum_{C\subseteq B}(-1)^{|B|-|C|}K_{C}.

By inclusion-exclusion, we have

K_{B}=\sum_{C\subseteq B}\eta_{C}.

The
descent-to-peak
map is

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}:&\mathrm{QSym}&\to&\Pi\\
&F_{(P,\gamma)}&\mapsto&K_{(P,\gamma)},\end{array}

which is a surjective morphism of graded Hopf algebras (see
[
9
]
).

When
(w,\gamma)
is a labelled chain with descent set
A
and peak set
B
, then

\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}(F_{A})=\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}(F_{(w,\gamma)})=K_{(w,\gamma)}=K_{B},

so
\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}
maps a fundamental basis element indexed by a descent set of a labelled chain to the enriched fundamental basis element indexed by the peak set of the same labelled chain. Consequently, the map
\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}
is called the descent-to-peak map.

Lemma 3.8
.

Given a labelled poset
(P,\gamma)
, let
G
be an edge-coloured digraph isomorphic to the Hasse diagram of
P
, and moreover,

(1)

a\Rightarrow b
in
G
if and and only if
b
covers
a
in
P
and
\gamma(a)\leq\gamma(b)
.

(2)

a\rightarrow b
in
G
if and and only if
b
covers
a
in
P
and
\gamma(a)>\gamma(b)
.

Then

(i)

the set of
(P,\gamma)
-partitions and the set of proper colourings of
G
are identical,

(ii)

F_{(P,\gamma)}={\mathscr{X}}_{G},

(iii)

the set of enriched
(P,\gamma)
-partitions and the set of enriched colourings of
G
are identical,

(iv)

K_{(P,\gamma)}={\mathscr{E}}_{G}.

The above lemma enables us to translate the results about
(P,\gamma)
-partitions and peak algebra into the language of generalized chromatic functions.

Corollary 3.9
.

The descent-to-peak map can be rewritten as

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\Theta_{\mathrm{QSym}}:&\mathrm{QSym}&\to&\Pi\\
&{\mathscr{X}}_{G}&\mapsto&{\mathscr{E}}_{G}.\end{array}

.

4.
Standard and enriched standard pairs

4.1.
Set compositions and standard pairs

Recall that a set composition
\phi
of
[n]
is a list of mutually disjoint nonempty subsets
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{\ell(\phi)}
of
[n]
whose union is
[n]
; this is denoted by
\phi_{1}|\phi_{2}|\cdots|\phi_{\ell(\phi)}\vDash[n]
.
Each
\phi_{i}
is called a
block
of the set composition
\phi
, and the
length
of
\phi
is
\ell(\phi)
. By convention, we denote by
\emptyset
the unique empty set composition of
[0]=\emptyset
. For example,
\phi=12|5|34\vDash[5]
has length 3.

Given a pair
(A,\sigma)
,
A=\{a_{1}<a_{2}<\dots<a_{k}\}\subseteq[n-1]
and
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
, define

\mathrm{SetComp}(A,\sigma)=\sigma(1)\cdots\sigma(a_{1})~|~\sigma(a_{1}+1)\cdots\sigma(a_{2})~|\cdots|~\sigma(a_{k})\cdots\sigma(n)\vDash[n].

For example,

\mathrm{SetComp}(\{2,5,6\},67325841)=67|325|8|41.

Let
A\subseteq[n-1]
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. Remember that the pair
(A,\sigma)
is standard if
\mathrm{Des}(\sigma)\subseteq A
, that is
a\notin A
implies
\sigma(a)<\sigma(a+1)
.
For example, when
A=\{2,5,6\}
and
\sigma=37124856
, then
(A,\sigma)
is standard and
\mathrm{SetComp}(A,\sigma)=37|124|8|56
.
Note that for any pair
(A,\sigma)
,
A\subseteq[n-1]
and
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
, there is a unique
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that
(A,\sigma^{\prime})
is standard and
\mathrm{SetComp}(A,\sigma)=\mathrm{SetComp}(A,\sigma^{\prime})
. We denote
(A,\sigma^{\prime})
by
\mathrm{Std}(A,\sigma)
.
For example,

\mathrm{Std}(\{3,5\},37284516)=(\{3,5\},23748156).

Proposition 4.1
.

The map

\begin{array}[]{cccc}&\{(A,\sigma):A\subseteq[n-1],\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n},(A,\sigma)~{\rm is~standard}\}&\rightarrow&\{\phi:\phi\vDash[n]\}\\
&(A,\sigma)&\mapsto&\mathrm{SetComp}(A,\sigma)\end{array}

is a bijection.

Proof.

We first show that the function
\mathrm{SetComp}
is surjective. Given a set composition
\phi=\phi_{1}|\phi_{2}|\dots|\phi_{\ell(\phi)}
, let
\phi_{ij}
be the
j
th smallest element in
\phi_{i}
. Let
k=\ell(\phi)-1
. Let
|\phi_{i}|=a_{i}-a_{i-1}
,
1\leq i\leq k+1
, with
a_{0}=0
and
a_{k+1}=n
.
Set

\sigma=\phi_{11}\phi_{12}\dots\phi_{1a_{1}}\phi_{21}\phi_{22}\dots\phi_{2(a_{2}-a_{1})}\dots\phi_{k1}\phi_{k2}\dots\phi_{k(n-a_{k})}

and

A=\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{k}\}

then

\mathrm{SetComp}(A,\sigma)=\phi_{11}\phi_{12}\dots\phi_{1a_{1}}~|~\phi_{21}\phi_{22}\dots\phi_{2(a_{2}-a_{1})}~|\dots|~\phi_{k1}\phi_{k2}\dots\phi_{k(n-a_{k})}=\phi.

Therefore, the function
\mathrm{SetComp}
is surjective.

Now we show that
\mathrm{SetComp}
is an injection. If
\phi=\mathrm{SetComp}(A,\sigma)=\mathrm{SetComp}(A^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
, since
A
and
A^{\prime}
identify the sizes of the blocks of
\phi
, we have that
A=A^{\prime}
. Moreover, by the definition of the function
\mathrm{SetComp}
, we must have
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{SetComp}
is injective.

∎

4.2.
Odd set compositions and enriched standard pairs

An
odd
set composition is a set composition whose all blocks have odd sizes. We say a pair
(A,\sigma)
,
A\subseteq[n-1]
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, is
odd
if
A
is an odd set. Therefore, for any odd pair
(A,\sigma)
,
{\rm SetComp}(A,\sigma)
is an odd set composition.

A pair
(B,\sigma)
,
B\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}
is a peak set and
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
, is called
enriched standard
if
(\mathrm{Odd}(B),\sigma)
is standard, that is
\mathrm{Des}(\sigma)\subseteq\mathrm{Odd}(B)
.
For example, when
B=\{2,5,7\}
and
\sigma=278134569
, then
(B,\sigma)
is enriched standard since

\mathrm{Des}(\sigma)=\{3\}\subseteq\mathrm{Odd}(B)=\{3,8\}.

Note that for any pair
(B,\sigma)
,
B\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-1\}
is a peak set and
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
, there is a unique
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
such that
(B,\sigma^{\prime})
is enriched standard. We denote
(B,\sigma^{\prime})
by
{\rm EStd}(B,\sigma)
. For example,

{\rm EStd}(\{2,5,7\},287134659)=(\{2,5,7\},278134569).

For each enriched standard pair
(B,\sigma)
, define

\mathrm{SetCompOdd}(B,\sigma)=\mathrm{SetComp}(\mathrm{Odd}(B),\sigma).

Proposition 4.2
.

The map from the set of enriched standard pairs
(B,\sigma)
,
B\subseteq[n-1]
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, to the set of odd set compositions of
[n]
given by

\begin{array}[]{cccc}&(B,\sigma)&\mapsto&\mathrm{SetCompOdd}(B,\sigma).\end{array}

is a bijection.

Proof.

To show that
\mathrm{SetCompOdd}
is a bijection, we only need to show that it is surjective. Given an odd set composition

\phi=\phi_{1}|\phi_{2}|\cdots|\phi_{\ell(\phi)},

let
B
be a peak set such that

\mathrm{Odd}(B)=\{|\phi_{1}|<|\phi_{1}|+|\phi_{2}|<\cdots<|\phi_{1}|+|\phi_{2}|+\cdots+|\phi_{\ell(\phi)-1}|\},

and let
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
be the permutation for which

\sigma(\{|\phi_{1}|+\cdots+|\phi_{i-1}|+1,|\phi_{1}|+\cdots+|\phi_{i-1}|+2,|\phi_{1}|+\cdots+|\phi_{i-1}|+|\phi_{i}|)={\mathrm{sort}}(\phi_{i})

for all
i
. Then

\mathrm{SetCompOdd}(B,\sigma)=\phi.

∎

5.
Fundamental functions in noncommuting variables and the analogue of the fundamental theorem

5.1.
Quasisymmetric functions in noncommuting variables

Recall that
{\mathbb{Q}}\langle\langle{\bf x}_{1},{\bf x}_{2},\dots\rangle\rangle
is the algebra of formal power series in infinitely many noncommuting variables
{\bf x}=\{{\bf x}_{1},{\bf x}_{2},\dots\}
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Given
(A,\sigma)
,
A\subseteq[n-1]
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, define the
monomial quasisymmetric function in noncommuting variables

{\mathbf{M}}_{(A,\sigma)}
to be

{\mathbf{M}}_{(A,\sigma)}=\sum{\bf x}_{i_{1}}{\bf x}_{i_{2}}\cdots{\bf x}_{i_{n}}

where the sum is over all tuples
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})
of positive integers such that

(1)

i_{j}=i_{k}
if and only if
j
and
k
are in the same block of
\mathrm{SetComp}(A,\sigma)
.

(2)

i_{j}<i_{k}
if and only if
j
is in a block of
\mathrm{SetComp}(A,\sigma)
to the left of the block containing
k
.

Note that for pairs
(A,\sigma_{1})
and
(A,\sigma_{2})
with
\mathrm{Std}(A,\sigma_{1})=\mathrm{Std}(A,\sigma_{2})
, we have

\mathbf{M}_{(A,\sigma_{1})}=\mathbf{M}_{(A,\sigma_{2})}.

The Hopf algebra of
quasisymmetric functions in noncommuting variables
is

\mathrm{NCQSym}=\displaystyle\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\mathrm{NCQSym}_{n}

where

\mathrm{NCQSym}_{n}=\mathbb{Q}\text{-span}\{\mathbf{M}_{(A,\sigma)}:A\subseteq[n-1],\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n},\text{~$(A,\sigma)$ is standard}\}.

The basis
\{\mathbf{M}_{(A,\sigma)}:\text{$(A,\sigma)$ is standard}\}
is called the
monomial basis
of the Hopf algebra of quasisymmetric functions in noncommuting variables.

5.2.
Fundamental functions in noncommuting variables

Given
(P,\gamma,\sigma)
where
(P,\gamma)
is a labelled poset with ground set
X
,
|X|=n
, and
\sigma:X\to[n]
is a bijection, define the
fundamental function in noncommuting variables

\mathbf{F}_{(P,\gamma,\sigma)}
to be

\mathbf{F}_{(P,\gamma,\sigma)}=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{A}(P,\gamma)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{x}_{f(\sigma^{-1}(i))}=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{A}(P,\gamma)}\mathbf{x}_{f(\sigma^{-1}(1))}\mathbf{x}_{f(\sigma^{-1}(2))}\cdots\mathbf{x}_{f(\sigma^{-1}(n))}.

\mathbf{F}_{(P,\gamma,\sigma)}=&{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{2}{\bf x}_{2}{\bf x}_{2}&+&{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{2}{\bf x}_{3}&+&{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{2}{\bf x}_{3}{\bf x}_{2}&+&{\bf x}_{1}{\bf x}_{2}{\bf x}_{3}{\bf x}_{3}&+\cdots.\end{array}

Definition 5.2
.

Let
(P,\gamma)
be a labelled poset with ground set
X
and
|X|=n
. Let
\sigma:X\to[n]
be a bijection. The labelled edge-coloured digraph
{\mathbf{G}}
associated to
(P,\gamma,\sigma)
is a labelled edge-coloured digraph
{\mathbf{G}}
such that

(1)

The vertex set of
{\mathbf{G}}
is
[n]
.

(2)

\sigma^{-1}(a)\Rightarrow\sigma^{-1}(b)
in
{\mathbf{G}}
if and only if
b
covers
a
in
P
and
\gamma(\sigma^{-1}(a))<\gamma(\sigma^{-1}(b))
.

(3)

\sigma^{-1}(a)\rightarrow\sigma^{-1}(b)
in
{\mathbf{G}}
if and only if
b
covers
a
in
P
and
\gamma(\sigma^{-1}(a))>\gamma(\sigma^{-1}(b))
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Given the labelled edge-coloured digraph
{\mathbf{G}}
associated to
(P,\gamma,\sigma)
, we have

{\mathbf{F}}_{(P,\gamma,\sigma)}={\mathscr{Y}}_{\mathbf{G}}.

Example 5.4
.

Given
(P,\gamma,\sigma)
in Example
5.1
,

{\mathbf{F}}_{(P,\gamma,\sigma)}={\mathscr{Y}}_{\mathbf{G}}

where
{\mathbf{G}}
is the following edge-coloured digraph.
{\mathbf{1}}
{\mathbf{2}}
{\mathbf{3}}
{\mathbf{4}}

5.3.
The analogue of the fundamental theorem

From now on
w=(w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{n})
is a chain with the ground set
\{w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{n}\}=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
. So we can look at any bijection
\sigma:\{w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{n}\}\rightarrow[n]
as a permutation in
{\mathfrak{S}}_{n}
.

Proposition 5.5
.

We have that

\mathbf{F}_{(P,\gamma,\sigma)}=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{L}(P)}\mathbf{F}_{(w,\gamma,\sigma)}.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.4
, we have
\mathcal{A}(P,\gamma)=\bigsqcup_{w\in\mathcal{L}(P)}\mathcal{A}(w,\gamma)
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{(P,\gamma,\sigma)}=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{A}(P,\gamma)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{x}_{f(\sigma^{-1}(i))}=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{L}(P)}\left(\sum_{f\in\mathcal{A}(w,\gamma)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{x}_{f(\sigma^{-1}(i))}\right)=\sum_{w\in\mathcal{L}(P)}\mathbf{F}_{(w,\gamma,\sigma)}.

∎

Proposition 5.6
.

We have that
\mathbf{F}_{(w,\gamma,\sigma)}=\mathbf{F}_{(w,\gamma^{\prime},\sigma)}
if and only if
\mathrm{Des}(w,\gamma)=\mathrm{Des}(w,\gamma^{\prime})
.

Proof.

If
\mathrm{Des}(w,\gamma)=\mathrm{Des}(w,\gamma^{\prime})
, by definition,
f\in\mathcal{A}(w,\gamma)
implies
f\in\mathcal{A}(w,\gamma^{\prime})
. Therefore,
\mathbf{F}_{(w,\gamma,\sigma)}=\mathbf{F}_{(w,\gamma^{\prime},\sigma)}
.

Conversely, if
{\mathbf{F}}_{(w,\gamma,\sigma)}={\mathbf{F}}_{(w,\gamma^{\prime},\sigma)}
, then commuting the variables we have
F_{(w,\gamma)}=F_{(w,\gamma)}
. Therefore,
\mathrm{Des}(w,\gamma)=\mathrm{Des}(w,\gamma^{\prime})
.
∎

Given
A\subseteq[n-1]
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, define

\mathbf{F}_{(A,\sigma)}=\mathbf{F}_{(w,\gamma,\sigma)}

where
(w,\gamma)
is any labelled chain with
\mathrm{Des}(w,\gamma)=A
.

Example 5.7
.

Let
w=(w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},w_{4},w_{5})
be a chain,

\gamma(w_{1})\gamma(w_{2})\gamma(w_{3})\gamma(w_{4})\gamma(w_{5})=34152,

and

\sigma(w_{1})\sigma(w_{2})\sigma(w_{3})\sigma(w_{4})\sigma(w_{5})=51423,

then

\mathrm{Des}(w,\gamma)=\{2,4\},

and so

{\mathbf{F}}_{(\{2,4\},\sigma)}={\mathbf{F}}_{(w,\gamma,\sigma)}.

The representation of
{\mathbf{F}}_{(\{2,4\},\sigma)}
in terms of generalized chromatic functions in noncommuting variables is shown below.

The set
\{2,4\}
clarifies the types of edges and the bijection
\sigma
provides the labels of the vertices.

5.4.
Fundamental functions to monomial basis

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
A\subseteq[n-1]
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. Then

\mathbf{F}_{(A,\sigma)}=\sum_{A\subseteq C\subseteq[n-1]}\mathbf{M}_{(C,\sigma)}.

Proof.

By definition,
\mathbf{F}_{(A,\sigma)}=\mathbf{F}_{(w,\gamma,\sigma)}
where
w=(1,2,\dots,n)
and
\mathrm{Des}(w,\gamma)=A
. Note the set of
(w,\gamma)
-partitions,
\mathcal{A}(w,\gamma)
, can be decomposed into

\mathcal{A}(w,\gamma)=\bigsqcup_{A\subseteq C\subseteq[n-1]}\mathcal{A}_{C}(w,\gamma),

where
\mathcal{A}_{C}(w,\gamma)
is the set of
(w,\gamma)
-partitions
f
such that
f(w_{i})<f(w_{i+1})
if and only if
i\in C
.
Therefore,

\mathbf{F}_{(A,\sigma)}=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{A}(w,\gamma)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{x}_{f(\sigma^{-1}(i))}=\sum_{A\subseteq C\subseteq[n-1]}\left(\sum_{f\in\mathcal{A}_{C}(w,\gamma)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{x}_{f(\sigma^{-1}(i))}\right).

For each
A\subseteq C\subseteq[n-1]
, we have

\sum_{f\in\mathcal{A}_{C}(w,\gamma)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{x}_{f(\sigma^{-1}(i))}=\mathbf{M}_{(C,\sigma)}.

Therefore,

\mathbf{F}_{(A,\sigma)}=\sum_{A\subseteq C\subseteq[n-1]}\left(\sum_{f\in\mathcal{A}_{C}(w,\gamma)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{x}_{f(\sigma^{-1}(i))}\right)=\sum_{A\subseteq C\subseteq[n-1]}\mathbf{M}_{(C,\sigma)}.

∎

Corollary 5.9
.

The set
\{\mathbf{F}_{(A,\sigma)}:(A,\sigma)\text{ is standard}\}
forms a basis of
\mathrm{NCQSym}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.1
we have

\mathbf{F}_{(A,\sigma)}=\sum_{A\subseteq C\subseteq[n-1]}\mathbf{M}_{(C,\sigma)}.

Note that
A\subseteq C
if and only if
\mathrm{SetComp}(A,\sigma)\leq\mathrm{SetComp}(C,\sigma)
. The result follows from triangularity and Proposition
4.1
.
∎

We call
\{\mathbf{F}_{(A,\sigma)}:(A,\sigma)\text{ is standard}\}
the
fundamental basis
of
\mathrm{NCQSym}
.

Remark 5.10
.

The fundamental basis also appears in
[
3
, Section 11]
.

6.
Enriched fundamental functions in noncommuting variables and the analogue of the fundamental theorem

6.1.
Enriched fundamental functions in noncommuting variables

In this section, we present the enriched fundamental functions in noncommuting variables.

Given
(P,\gamma,\sigma)
where
(P,\gamma)
is a labelled poset with ground set
X
,
|X|=n
, and
\sigma:X\to[n]
is a bijection, define the
enriched fundamental function in noncommuting variables

\mathbf{K}_{(P,\gamma,\sigma)}
to be

\mathbf{K}_{(P,\gamma,\sigma)}=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{B}(P,\gamma)}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{x}_{|f(\sigma^{-1}(i))|}=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{B}(P,\gamma)}\mathbf{x}_{|f(\sigma^{-1}(1))|}\mathbf{x}_{|f(\sigma^{-1}(2))|}\dots\mathbf{x}_{|f(\sigma^{-1}(n))|}.

.

,
